The mechanism is EIP-7702, shipped in the Pectra\nupgrade, live on Ethereum mainnet since 2025-05-07 10:05:11 UTC. No new address,\nno new key, no migration.**\n\nEverything below is checked against mainnet, not recalled. Two evidence sources:\n\n- **Live mainnet reads** via `cast` against `https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com`\n  (chain id `1`), at head block `25,623,659`, timestamp `1785149471` =\n  `2026-07-27T10:51:11Z`.\n- **A working end-to-end demo** on an `anvil` fork of mainnet at block\n  `25,623,699`, which actually delegates an EOA, runs an atomic\n  approve+swap through Uniswap V2, drives it from a session key, and then\n  revokes. Script: `demo/run.sh`, full output: `demo/transcript.txt`.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Is it possible today, and what makes it possible?\n\nYes.\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| Mechanism | **EIP-7702** — a new transaction type, `0x04` (`SetCodeTransaction`), carrying an `authorization_list`. A signed authorization sets a *delegation designator* in the signing account's code field. |\n| Upgrade | **Pectra** (Prague execution layer + Electra consensus layer) |\n| Mainnet activation | **2025-05-07, 10:05:11 UTC**, execution block **22,431,084** |\n\n### How I got the activation date\n\nPectra activates by beacon epoch, so the timestamp is derived, then confirmed\nagainst the chain:\n\n```\nbeacon genesis        = 1606824023\nPectra epoch          = 364032\nslot                  = 364032 * 32           = 11,649,024\ntimestamp             = 1606824023 + 11649024*12 = 1,746,612,311\n                                              -> 2025-05-07T10:05:11Z\n```\n\nConfirmed on mainnet — 22,431,084 is the first block at that exact timestamp:\n\n```\nblock 22431084  ts 1746612311  2025-05-07T10:05:11+00:00   <- first Pectra block\nblock 22431083  ts 1746612299  2025-05-07T10:04:59+00:00   <- last pre-Pectra block\n```\n\n### It is not theoretical — it is in every block right now\n\nSampling six consecutive recent mainnet blocks for `type == 0x4` transactions:\n\n| block | total txs | type-0x04 txs |\n|---|---|---|\n| 25,623,650 | 205 | 6 |\n| 25,623,649 | 193 | 4 |\n| 25,623,648 | 242 | 4 |\n| 25,623,647 | 220 | 6 |\n| 25,623,646 | 225 | 7 |\n| 25,623,645 | 237 | 4 |\n\n### The account does not become a new account type\n\nThis is the crux of the requirement. The account keeps its address, its nonce\nsequence, its balance, and its ECDSA key. It is still an EOA — it can still send\nordinary type-2 transactions signed by the same key — it just now *also* has\ncode. A live mainnet example that is exactly your users' situation:\n\n```\n0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  ENS reverse   ozyart.eth\n  ENS forward   ozyart.eth -> 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  nonce         406                      <- long history, unchanged\n  balance       1071103547166 wei\n  code          0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be\n```\n\nAn ENS-named account with 406 transactions of history that is running contract\ncode today, at the same address. That is the deliverable, in production.\n\n---\n\n## 2. What the account points at, and how approve+swap becomes atomic\n\n### The code field\n\nAfter opting in, `eth_getCode(user)` returns **exactly 23 bytes**:\n\n```\n0xef01  00        <20-byte address>\nMAGIC   version   delegate implementation\n```\n\nThree real mainnet accounts, decoded:\n\n| account | code | delegate |\n|---|---|---|\n| `0x864acf04…12f60` | `0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be` | `0x923906ca…04be` |\n| `0x5ffcd471…f134` | `0xef010047a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` | `0x47a51af4…fb1c` |\n| `0x88753814…a5cd` | `0xef0100490aac77c960b0569c8e446ac7e12490bd44ca1d` | `0x490aac77…ca1d` |\n\nThe 20 bytes are a *pointer*, not a copy. The implementation is deployed once and\nshared by every user; each user's account stores 23 bytes.\n\n### How the delegation gets there\n\nThe user signs an authorization tuple with their existing key. The digest is:\n\n```\nkeccak256( 0x05 || rlp([chain_id, delegate_address, nonce]) )\n```\n\nI verified the `0x05` magic and the signer binding by recovering the authority\nfrom a real mainnet authorization by hand (pure-python secp256k1, no libraries —\n`demo/recover-authority.py`), using tx\n`0x0b46ca32de6643b80a5c4440e41898b7fa5f97d9c5dae9bece8b8144adb31141`:\n\n```\nrlp        0xd70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\npreimage   0x05d70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\ndigest     0xdf05bbf2bad4c4bd70b797e4bdf4f54c05efc6bff178f1edf0e3f3e138780f29\nrecovered  0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b\nexpected   0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b   ✓\n```\n\nSo: only the account's own key can set or change its delegation. Nobody can push\ncode onto a user's account.\n\n### Where the code runs\n\nCalls into the account resolve the pointer and execute the delegate's bytecode\n**in the account's own context**:\n\n- `address(this)` == the user's address\n- `SLOAD`/`SSTORE` hit the user's storage\n- outbound `CALL`s carry `msg.sender` == the user's address\n\nThat last point is what makes approve+swap work: the ERC-20 `approve` is recorded\nagainst the user's address, and the router sees the user as the spender. The\ndelegate needs nothing more than a loop:\n\n```solidity\nfunction execute(Call[] calldata calls) external payable {\n    require(msg.sender == address(this));     // the account's own key signed the tx\n    for (uint256 i; i < calls.length; ++i) {\n        (bool ok, bytes memory ret) = calls[i].to.call{value: calls[i].value}(calls[i].data);\n        if (!ok) { assembly { revert(add(ret, 32), mload(ret)) } }   // -> atomic\n    }\n}\n```\n\n`require(msg.sender == address(this))` is the self-call check: the user sends a\nnormal transaction *to their own address*, so `msg.sender == address(this) ==\nthe EOA`. This same shape is already deployed on mainnet — e.g. delegate\n`0x47a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` exposes selector `0x34fcd5be` =\n`execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])`.\n\n### The demo (fork of mainnet, real Uniswap V2)\n\nUser account `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b`, given 5,000 USDC and\nsome plain-transfer history first (nonce 3, code `0x`).\n\n**Opt-in** — one type-`0x04` transaction signed by the user's own key:\n\n```\ntx type      0x4\nauth tuple   chainId 0x1  address 0x3d72617b…4b78  nonce 0x4\ngasUsed      36844\ncode after   0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  bytes 23 | magic 0xef01 | version 0x0 | points at 0x3d72617b…4b78  (matches: True)\nnonce        3 -> 5     (tx consumed nonce 3, the authorization consumed nonce 4)\nusdc         5000000000  unchanged\n```\n\n**The batch** — one transaction, both actions:\n\n```\nbatch tx  0x570a9aca9fc8ff393c3d0019e00cbfa5847304e178b62d8d04350d9b28e32430\nstatus 0x1   gasUsed 142843   logs 6\n  USDC   0x8c5be1e5…  Approval\n  USDC   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (user -> pair)\n  WETH   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (pair -> user)\n  pair   0x1c411e9a…  Sync\n  pair   0xd78ad95f…  Swap\n  user   0x91571a8e…  Batched\nusdc 5000000000 -> 4000000000\nweth          0 -> 507617031447445491\n```\n\nOne hash, both events, and the last log is emitted **by the user's own address**.\n\n**Atomicity, proved by breaking it** — same batch with an unreachable\n`amountOutMin`:\n\n```\nbatch reverted as expected\nusdc before 4000000000\nusdc after  4000000000    <- the approve rolled back with it\n```\n\n**Session key / custom auth** — the delegate stores an expiry in the *user's*\nstorage, and a completely separate keypair then spends from the user's account:\n\n```\nsession key   0xCF6355a9bC38826437391f55fc3423D8B9E56cF3   (not the user's key)\nexpiry read from the user's account: 1785153557\nsession-key tx status 0x1\n  ERC-20 Transfer topic1 = 0x000000000000000000000000 17975634267e889bf0ebb43e4937e552cedb7d1b\n```\n\nThe tokens moved *from the user's address*, authorized by a key that is not the\nuser's key. That is the second requirement, done.\n\n### Cost of opting in\n\nThe mainnet revocation tx above used exactly **36,800 gas**, which reconstructs\ncleanly from the spec constants:\n\n```\n21,000  intrinsic\n+25,000  PER_EMPTY_ACCOUNT_COST (1 authorization)\n=46,000\n -9,200  refund, capped by EIP-3529 at gas_used/5 = 46,000/5\n         (the uncapped refund would be 25,000 - 12,500 = 12,500)\n=36,800  ✓ matches the receipt\n```\n\nThe fork's 36,844 is 44 gas more because there the call fell through into the\nfreshly-installed delegate's `receive()`. Budget ~37k gas of one-time onboarding\nper user; it can be sponsored by a relayer, since the authorization signer and\nthe transaction sender do not have to be the same account.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Does it wear off after one batch? No.\n\n**The delegation is persistent account state.** It is stored in the account's code\nfield and stays there indefinitely — across that transaction, across every later\ntransaction, across upgrades. There is no expiry, no counter, no auto-revert.\n\nFrom the demo, after the batch already ran:\n\n```\n=== 6. does it wear off? run a SECOND batch with no new authorization ===\n  code still  0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  second batch OK, no auth tuple in that tx\n  weth now    761413698327188028      (increased again)\n```\n\n### Exactly what is required to change or remove it\n\n**A new type-`0x04` transaction carrying a fresh authorization tuple signed by\nthat same account's key.** Nothing else does it. Specifically:\n\n- **Re-point to a different implementation** — authorize a new address. The 23\n  bytes are overwritten.\n- **Remove it entirely** — authorize `0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000000`.\n  The code field goes back to empty and the account is a plain EOA again.\n- Each authorization **consumes one nonce** of the authorizing account, and is\n  bound to a specific `(chain_id, delegate, nonce)`, so it is not replayable.\n\nAll three states observed on one real mainnet account,\n`0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b` (nonce 121), within ten blocks:\n\n| block | `eth_getCode` |\n|---|---|\n| 25,623,640 | `0xef0100fc2c64ce1c098a7328874dd09d9a71e306d1a948` |\n| 25,623,649 | `0xef010088cf071b4bf5facab6712070a671f71282188d46` ← re-pointed |\n| 25,623,650 | `0x` ← cleared, by tx `0x0b46ca32…31141` authorizing `address(0)` |\n\nAnd in the demo:\n\n```\n=== 8. removing it: another SetCode tx, this time to the zero address ===\n  code now    0x\n  same batch calldata -> status 0x1 logs 0 (no code ran)\n  weth before 761413698327188028\n  weth after  761413698327188028     <- unchanged, batching is gone\n```\n\nNote the trap in that last block: sending batch calldata to a codeless account\n**does not revert** — there is no code to run, so it silently succeeds as an inert\nno-op. Check effects, not `status`, when you test revocation.\n\nFinal state of the demo account, start to finish: same address, same key, nonce\nadvanced normally to 11, balances intact, and plain EOA transfers still work.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Why the fresh ERC-4337 wallet does not satisfy the constraint\n\nIt fails on the one requirement that was stated as non-negotiable: **the address\nchanges.** ERC-4337 accounts are contracts. A contract must be deployed, and a\ndeployed contract's address is determined by the deployer and the init code, not\nby the user's key.\n\nReal mainnet factories, asked for the counterfactual account address for the\ndemo user's own key as owner:\n\n| factory | resulting account address |\n|---|---|\n| `0x91E60e0613810449d098b0b5Ec8b51A0FE8c8985` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.7) | `0x333DD74044d78D4E97e8dd45f263Cc698feB2672` |\n| `0x9406Cc6185a346906296840746125a0E44976454` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.6) | `0xfAAa2e112ef9968e265d833F05BB7FD47A48Cbd6` |\n| the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |\n\nSame key, three different addresses, none of them the user's. That is structural,\nnot a configuration mistake:\n\n```\naccount = keccak256(0xff ++ factory ++ salt ++ keccak256(initCode))[12:]\n```\n\nMaking that land on a pre-existing EOA address would be a preimage attack on\nkeccak-256. And even if it landed there, the user's private key would not control\nthe resulting contract — a 4337 account is controlled by whatever validation\nlogic its code implements, keyed off storage, not by being the ECDSA preimage of\nits own address.\n\nSo \"move funds and ENS across\" is the honest description of the plan, and it\nmeans:\n\n- **Address**: gone. Every counterparty, contract, allowlist, or off-chain system\n  that has the old address hardcoded now points at the wrong place.\n- **ENS**: the name can be re-pointed to the new address (`setAddr`), but that is a\n  migration with a cost, a window of inconsistency, and it requires each user to\n  act. The *reverse* record must be re-set from the new address too. Users who\n  don't complete it are stranded.\n- **History**: does not move. Ever. Onchain history is indexed by address. A\n  year of activity, reputation, and provenance is orphaned at the old address.\n- **Everything non-transferable**: airdrop snapshots already taken against the old\n  address, soulbound tokens, POAPs, allowlist spots, locked/vesting positions,\n  governance delegation and voting power history, credit or reputation scores.\n  These cannot be transferred at all.\n- **Operationally**: N users × (deploy + approve-revoke + n token transfers + NFT\n  transfers + ENS updates), each a chance to lose funds, each requiring the user\n  to show up and sign. Compare to one ~37k-gas authorization, which you can\n  sponsor.\n\n### The constructive version of the teammate's idea\n\nThey are not wrong that they want 4337's infrastructure — bundlers, paymasters,\nsponsored gas. They don't have to give up the address to get it. **Point the\n7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible account implementation.** EntryPoint v0.8\n(`0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108`, 21,738 bytes of code) explicitly\nsupports 7702 authorities, and this is already running on mainnet — e.g. block\n25,623,649 tx `0xaeec5480…c3155`, a type-`0x04` transaction *to* that EntryPoint\ncarrying an authorization. Delegate `0xd2e28229f6f2c235e57de2ebc727025a1d0530fb`\nimplements both `execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])` (`0x34fcd5be`) and\n`execute(address,uint256,bytes)` (`0xb61d27f6`) and is used with EntryPoint v0.7.\n\nSame address, same key, and the whole 4337 stack.\n\n---\n\n## Things to flag before you ship\n\n1. **Set `chain_id` to `1`, not `0`.** An authorization with `chain_id = 0` is\n   valid on *every* chain and can be replayed anywhere your users have that\n   address. Every live authorization I sampled uses `chainId 0x1`.\n2. **Re-pointing does not clear storage.** The account's storage survives a change\n   of delegate (mainnet account `0x8339…672b` above did exactly this). If v2 of\n   your implementation lays out storage differently, you will read v1's data. Use\n   ERC-7201 namespaced slots from day one.\n3. **These accounts now look like contracts.** `EXTCODESIZE > 0` and\n   `msg.sender != tx.origin`-style checks will start treating your users as\n   contracts. Some dapps, token gates, and anti-bot filters will reject them.\n   Test the flows your users actually run before rolling out broadly.\n4. **The delegate is total authority over the account.** It runs with the user's\n   full balance and permissions. Malicious 7702 delegations are the live drainer\n   pattern — one of the transactions I sampled while researching this carried\n   ~120 authorization tuples in a single transaction, mass-delegating many\n   accounts at once. Pin one audited implementation, don't let users sign\n   arbitrary authorizations from your UI, and put the session-key expiry and\n   spend limits in the delegate itself.\n5. **Users can always exit.** Authorizing `address(0)` returns the account to a\n   plain EOA. Give them that button.\n\n---\n\n## Reproducing this\n\n```bash\n# live mainnet reads\ncast code 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\ncast lookup-address 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\n\n# the end-to-end demo\nanvil --fork-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com --port 8546 --silent &\ncd demo && forge build && bash run.sh\n```\n\n- `demo/src/BatchDelegate.sol` — the delegate implementation (batch + session keys)\n- `demo/run.sh` — the nine-step demo\n- `demo/transcript.txt` — the full recorded output referenced above\n- `demo/recover-authority.py` — hand recovery of the authority from a live mainnet\n  authorization signature\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null,"userModified":false}}

(file state is current in your context — no need to Read it back)"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a20f0abb-7a52-4374-8736-89a1b43dbb01","uuid":"ae11d71e-4dfa-4af5-93c8-b8b55b2f0d38","timestamp":"2026-07-27T11:03:04.113Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/Users/liana/Documents/prog/buidl-guidl/ethskills-evals/artifacts/standards-quiz-002/2026-07-27T105037Z-claude-no-skill-3/workspace/answer.md","oldString":"| the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |\n\nSame key, three different addresses, none of them the user's.","newString":"| the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |\n\nThe EntryPoint versions above aren't taken on faith — each factory's\n`accountImplementation()` was called, then that implementation's `entryPoint()`:\n`0x91E60e06…` → `0x0000000071727De22E5E9d8BAf0edAc6f37da032` (v0.7), and\n`0x9406Cc61…` → `0x5FF137D4b0FDCD49DcA30c7CF57E578a026d2789` (v0.6).\n\nSame key, three different addresses, none of them the user's.","originalFile":"# EOAs that batch and run custom auth, same address, same key\n\n**Short answer: yes, ship it. The mechanism is EIP-7702, shipped in the Pectra\nupgrade, live on Ethereum mainnet since 2025-05-07 10:05:11 UTC. No new address,\nno new key, no migration.**\n\nEverything below is checked against mainnet, not recalled. Two evidence sources:\n\n- **Live mainnet reads** via `cast` against `https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com`\n  (chain id `1`), at head block `25,623,659`, timestamp `1785149471` =\n  `2026-07-27T10:51:11Z`.\n- **A working end-to-end demo** on an `anvil` fork of mainnet at block\n  `25,623,699`, which actually delegates an EOA, runs an atomic\n  approve+swap through Uniswap V2, drives it from a session key, and then\n  revokes. Script: `demo/run.sh`, full output: `demo/transcript.txt`.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Is it possible today, and what makes it possible?\n\nYes.\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| Mechanism | **EIP-7702** — a new transaction type, `0x04` (`SetCodeTransaction`), carrying an `authorization_list`. A signed authorization sets a *delegation designator* in the signing account's code field. |\n| Upgrade | **Pectra** (Prague execution layer + Electra consensus layer) |\n| Mainnet activation | **2025-05-07, 10:05:11 UTC**, execution block **22,431,084** |\n\n### How I got the activation date\n\nPectra activates by beacon epoch, so the timestamp is derived, then confirmed\nagainst the chain:\n\n```\nbeacon genesis        = 1606824023\nPectra epoch          = 364032\nslot                  = 364032 * 32           = 11,649,024\ntimestamp             = 1606824023 + 11649024*12 = 1,746,612,311\n                                              -> 2025-05-07T10:05:11Z\n```\n\nConfirmed on mainnet — 22,431,084 is the first block at that exact timestamp:\n\n```\nblock 22431084  ts 1746612311  2025-05-07T10:05:11+00:00   <- first Pectra block\nblock 22431083  ts 1746612299  2025-05-07T10:04:59+00:00   <- last pre-Pectra block\n```\n\n### It is not theoretical — it is in every block right now\n\nSampling six consecutive recent mainnet blocks for `type == 0x4` transactions:\n\n| block | total txs | type-0x04 txs |\n|---|---|---|\n| 25,623,650 | 205 | 6 |\n| 25,623,649 | 193 | 4 |\n| 25,623,648 | 242 | 4 |\n| 25,623,647 | 220 | 6 |\n| 25,623,646 | 225 | 7 |\n| 25,623,645 | 237 | 4 |\n\n### The account does not become a new account type\n\nThis is the crux of the requirement. The account keeps its address, its nonce\nsequence, its balance, and its ECDSA key. It is still an EOA — it can still send\nordinary type-2 transactions signed by the same key — it just now *also* has\ncode. A live mainnet example that is exactly your users' situation:\n\n```\n0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  ENS reverse   ozyart.eth\n  ENS forward   ozyart.eth -> 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  nonce         406                      <- long history, unchanged\n  balance       1071103547166 wei\n  code          0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be\n```\n\nAn ENS-named account with 406 transactions of history that is running contract\ncode today, at the same address. That is the deliverable, in production.\n\n---\n\n## 2. What the account points at, and how approve+swap becomes atomic\n\n### The code field\n\nAfter opting in, `eth_getCode(user)` returns **exactly 23 bytes**:\n\n```\n0xef01  00        <20-byte address>\nMAGIC   version   delegate implementation\n```\n\nThree real mainnet accounts, decoded:\n\n| account | code | delegate |\n|---|---|---|\n| `0x864acf04…12f60` | `0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be` | `0x923906ca…04be` |\n| `0x5ffcd471…f134` | `0xef010047a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` | `0x47a51af4…fb1c` |\n| `0x88753814…a5cd` | `0xef0100490aac77c960b0569c8e446ac7e12490bd44ca1d` | `0x490aac77…ca1d` |\n\nThe 20 bytes are a *pointer*, not a copy. The implementation is deployed once and\nshared by every user; each user's account stores 23 bytes.\n\n### How the delegation gets there\n\nThe user signs an authorization tuple with their existing key. The digest is:\n\n```\nkeccak256( 0x05 || rlp([chain_id, delegate_address, nonce]) )\n```\n\nI verified the `0x05` magic and the signer binding by recovering the authority\nfrom a real mainnet authorization by hand (pure-python secp256k1, no libraries —\n`demo/recover-authority.py`), using tx\n`0x0b46ca32de6643b80a5c4440e41898b7fa5f97d9c5dae9bece8b8144adb31141`:\n\n```\nrlp        0xd70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\npreimage   0x05d70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\ndigest     0xdf05bbf2bad4c4bd70b797e4bdf4f54c05efc6bff178f1edf0e3f3e138780f29\nrecovered  0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b\nexpected   0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b   ✓\n```\n\nSo: only the account's own key can set or change its delegation. Nobody can push\ncode onto a user's account.\n\n### Where the code runs\n\nCalls into the account resolve the pointer and execute the delegate's bytecode\n**in the account's own context**:\n\n- `address(this)` == the user's address\n- `SLOAD`/`SSTORE` hit the user's storage\n- outbound `CALL`s carry `msg.sender` == the user's address\n\nThat last point is what makes approve+swap work: the ERC-20 `approve` is recorded\nagainst the user's address, and the router sees the user as the spender. The\ndelegate needs nothing more than a loop:\n\n```solidity\nfunction execute(Call[] calldata calls) external payable {\n    require(msg.sender == address(this));     // the account's own key signed the tx\n    for (uint256 i; i < calls.length; ++i) {\n        (bool ok, bytes memory ret) = calls[i].to.call{value: calls[i].value}(calls[i].data);\n        if (!ok) { assembly { revert(add(ret, 32), mload(ret)) } }   // -> atomic\n    }\n}\n```\n\n`require(msg.sender == address(this))` is the self-call check: the user sends a\nnormal transaction *to their own address*, so `msg.sender == address(this) ==\nthe EOA`. This same shape is already deployed on mainnet — e.g. delegate\n`0x47a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` exposes selector `0x34fcd5be` =\n`execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])`.\n\n### The demo (fork of mainnet, real Uniswap V2)\n\nUser account `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b`, given 5,000 USDC and\nsome plain-transfer history first (nonce 3, code `0x`).\n\n**Opt-in** — one type-`0x04` transaction signed by the user's own key:\n\n```\ntx type      0x4\nauth tuple   chainId 0x1  address 0x3d72617b…4b78  nonce 0x4\ngasUsed      36844\ncode after   0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  bytes 23 | magic 0xef01 | version 0x0 | points at 0x3d72617b…4b78  (matches: True)\nnonce        3 -> 5     (tx consumed nonce 3, the authorization consumed nonce 4)\nusdc         5000000000  unchanged\n```\n\n**The batch** — one transaction, both actions:\n\n```\nbatch tx  0x570a9aca9fc8ff393c3d0019e00cbfa5847304e178b62d8d04350d9b28e32430\nstatus 0x1   gasUsed 142843   logs 6\n  USDC   0x8c5be1e5…  Approval\n  USDC   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (user -> pair)\n  WETH   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (pair -> user)\n  pair   0x1c411e9a…  Sync\n  pair   0xd78ad95f…  Swap\n  user   0x91571a8e…  Batched\nusdc 5000000000 -> 4000000000\nweth          0 -> 507617031447445491\n```\n\nOne hash, both events, and the last log is emitted **by the user's own address**.\n\n**Atomicity, proved by breaking it** — same batch with an unreachable\n`amountOutMin`:\n\n```\nbatch reverted as expected\nusdc before 4000000000\nusdc after  4000000000    <- the approve rolled back with it\n```\n\n**Session key / custom auth** — the delegate stores an expiry in the *user's*\nstorage, and a completely separate keypair then spends from the user's account:\n\n```\nsession key   0xCF6355a9bC38826437391f55fc3423D8B9E56cF3   (not the user's key)\nexpiry read from the user's account: 1785153557\nsession-key tx status 0x1\n  ERC-20 Transfer topic1 = 0x000000000000000000000000 17975634267e889bf0ebb43e4937e552cedb7d1b\n```\n\nThe tokens moved *from the user's address*, authorized by a key that is not the\nuser's key. That is the second requirement, done.\n\n### Cost of opting in\n\nThe mainnet revocation tx above used exactly **36,800 gas**, which reconstructs\ncleanly from the spec constants:\n\n```\n21,000  intrinsic\n+25,000  PER_EMPTY_ACCOUNT_COST (1 authorization)\n=46,000\n -9,200  refund, capped by EIP-3529 at gas_used/5 = 46,000/5\n         (the uncapped refund would be 25,000 - 12,500 = 12,500)\n=36,800  ✓ matches the receipt\n```\n\nThe fork's 36,844 is 44 gas more because there the call fell through into the\nfreshly-installed delegate's `receive()`. Budget ~37k gas of one-time onboarding\nper user; it can be sponsored by a relayer, since the authorization signer and\nthe transaction sender do not have to be the same account.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Does it wear off after one batch? No.\n\n**The delegation is persistent account state.** It is stored in the account's code\nfield and stays there indefinitely — across that transaction, across every later\ntransaction, across upgrades. There is no expiry, no counter, no auto-revert.\n\nFrom the demo, after the batch already ran:\n\n```\n=== 6. does it wear off? run a SECOND batch with no new authorization ===\n  code still  0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  second batch OK, no auth tuple in that tx\n  weth now    761413698327188028      (increased again)\n```\n\n### Exactly what is required to change or remove it\n\n**A new type-`0x04` transaction carrying a fresh authorization tuple signed by\nthat same account's key.** Nothing else does it. Specifically:\n\n- **Re-point to a different implementation** — authorize a new address. The 23\n  bytes are overwritten.\n- **Remove it entirely** — authorize `0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000000`.\n  The code field goes back to empty and the account is a plain EOA again.\n- Each authorization **consumes one nonce** of the authorizing account, and is\n  bound to a specific `(chain_id, delegate, nonce)`, so it is not replayable.\n\nAll three states observed on one real mainnet account,\n`0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b` (nonce 121), within ten blocks:\n\n| block | `eth_getCode` |\n|---|---|\n| 25,623,640 | `0xef0100fc2c64ce1c098a7328874dd09d9a71e306d1a948` |\n| 25,623,649 | `0xef010088cf071b4bf5facab6712070a671f71282188d46` ← re-pointed |\n| 25,623,650 | `0x` ← cleared, by tx `0x0b46ca32…31141` authorizing `address(0)` |\n\nAnd in the demo:\n\n```\n=== 8. removing it: another SetCode tx, this time to the zero address ===\n  code now    0x\n  same batch calldata -> status 0x1 logs 0 (no code ran)\n  weth before 761413698327188028\n  weth after  761413698327188028     <- unchanged, batching is gone\n```\n\nNote the trap in that last block: sending batch calldata to a codeless account\n**does not revert** — there is no code to run, so it silently succeeds as an inert\nno-op. Check effects, not `status`, when you test revocation.\n\nFinal state of the demo account, start to finish: same address, same key, nonce\nadvanced normally to 11, balances intact, and plain EOA transfers still work.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Why the fresh ERC-4337 wallet does not satisfy the constraint\n\nIt fails on the one requirement that was stated as non-negotiable: **the address\nchanges.** ERC-4337 accounts are contracts. A contract must be deployed, and a\ndeployed contract's address is determined by the deployer and the init code, not\nby the user's key.\n\nReal mainnet factories, asked for the counterfactual account address for the\ndemo user's own key as owner:\n\n| factory | resulting account address |\n|---|---|\n| `0x91E60e0613810449d098b0b5Ec8b51A0FE8c8985` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.7) | `0x333DD74044d78D4E97e8dd45f263Cc698feB2672` |\n| `0x9406Cc6185a346906296840746125a0E44976454` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.6) | `0xfAAa2e112ef9968e265d833F05BB7FD47A48Cbd6` |\n| the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |\n\nSame key, three different addresses, none of them the user's. That is structural,\nnot a configuration mistake:\n\n```\naccount = keccak256(0xff ++ factory ++ salt ++ keccak256(initCode))[12:]\n```\n\nMaking that land on a pre-existing EOA address would be a preimage attack on\nkeccak-256. And even if it landed there, the user's private key would not control\nthe resulting contract — a 4337 account is controlled by whatever validation\nlogic its code implements, keyed off storage, not by being the ECDSA preimage of\nits own address.\n\nSo \"move funds and ENS across\" is the honest description of the plan, and it\nmeans:\n\n- **Address**: gone. Every counterparty, contract, allowlist, or off-chain system\n  that has the old address hardcoded now points at the wrong place.\n- **ENS**: the name can be re-pointed to the new address (`setAddr`), but that is a\n  migration with a cost, a window of inconsistency, and it requires each user to\n  act. The *reverse* record must be re-set from the new address too. Users who\n  don't complete it are stranded.\n- **History**: does not move. Ever. Onchain history is indexed by address. A\n  year of activity, reputation, and provenance is orphaned at the old address.\n- **Everything non-transferable**: airdrop snapshots already taken against the old\n  address, soulbound tokens, POAPs, allowlist spots, locked/vesting positions,\n  governance delegation and voting power history, credit or reputation scores.\n  These cannot be transferred at all.\n- **Operationally**: N users × (deploy + approve-revoke + n token transfers + NFT\n  transfers + ENS updates), each a chance to lose funds, each requiring the user\n  to show up and sign. Compare to one ~37k-gas authorization, which you can\n  sponsor.\n\n### The constructive version of the teammate's idea\n\nThey are not wrong that they want 4337's infrastructure — bundlers, paymasters,\nsponsored gas. They don't have to give up the address to get it. **Point the\n7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible account implementation.** EntryPoint v0.8\n(`0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108`, 21,738 bytes of code) explicitly\nsupports 7702 authorities, and this is already running on mainnet — e.g. block\n25,623,649 tx `0xaeec5480…c3155`, a type-`0x04` transaction *to* that EntryPoint\ncarrying an authorization. Delegate `0xd2e28229f6f2c235e57de2ebc727025a1d0530fb`\nimplements both `execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])` (`0x34fcd5be`) and\n`execute(address,uint256,bytes)` (`0xb61d27f6`) and is used with EntryPoint v0.7.\n\nSame address, same key, and the whole 4337 stack.\n\n---\n\n## Things to flag before you ship\n\n1. **Set `chain_id` to `1`, not `0`.** An authorization with `chain_id = 0` is\n   valid on *every* chain and can be replayed anywhere your users have that\n   address. Every live authorization I sampled uses `chainId 0x1`.\n2. **Re-pointing does not clear storage.** The account's storage survives a change\n   of delegate (mainnet account `0x8339…672b` above did exactly this). If v2 of\n   your implementation lays out storage differently, you will read v1's data. Use\n   ERC-7201 namespaced slots from day one.\n3. **These accounts now look like contracts.** `EXTCODESIZE > 0` and\n   `msg.sender != tx.origin`-style checks will start treating your users as\n   contracts. Some dapps, token gates, and anti-bot filters will reject them.\n   Test the flows your users actually run before rolling out broadly.\n4. **The delegate is total authority over the account.** It runs with the user's\n   full balance and permissions. Malicious 7702 delegations are the live drainer\n   pattern — one of the transactions I sampled while researching this carried\n   ~120 authorization tuples in a single transaction, mass-delegating many\n   accounts at once. Pin one audited implementation, don't let users sign\n   arbitrary authorizations from your UI, and put the session-key expiry and\n   spend limits in the delegate itself.\n5. **Users can always exit.** Authorizing `address(0)` returns the account to a\n   plain EOA. Give them that button.\n\n---\n\n## Reproducing this\n\n```bash\n# live mainnet reads\ncast code 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\ncast lookup-address 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\n\n# the end-to-end demo\nanvil --fork-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com --port 8546 --silent &\ncd demo && forge build && bash run.sh\n```\n\n- `demo/src/BatchDelegate.sol` — the delegate implementation (batch + session keys)\n- `demo/run.sh` — the nine-step demo\n- `demo/transcript.txt` — the full recorded output referenced above\n- `demo/recover-authority.py` — hand recovery of the authority from a live mainnet\n  authorization signature\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":303,"oldLines":6,"newStart":303,"newLines":11,"lines":[" | `0x9406Cc6185a346906296840746125a0E44976454` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.6) | `0xfAAa2e112ef9968e265d833F05BB7FD47A48Cbd6` |"," | the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |"," ","+The EntryPoint versions above aren't taken on faith — each factory's","+`accountImplementation()` was called, then that implementation's `entryPoint()`:","+`0x91E60e06…` → `0x0000000071727De22E5E9d8BAf0edAc6f37da032` (v0.7), and","+`0x9406Cc61…` → `0x5FF137D4b0FDCD49DcA30c7CF57E578a026d2789` (v0.6).","+"," Same key, three different addresses, none of them the user's. That is structural,"," not a configuration mistake:"," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

(file state is current in your context — no need to Read it back)"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a20f0abb-7a52-4374-8736-89a1b43dbb01","uuid":"f5cbe85d-f5e1-4516-8a2c-9b4e0c82b1f2","timestamp":"2026-07-27T11:03:10.395Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/Users/liana/Documents/prog/buidl-guidl/ethskills-evals/artifacts/standards-quiz-002/2026-07-27T105037Z-claude-no-skill-3/workspace/answer.md","oldString":"7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible account implementation.** EntryPoint v0.8\n(`0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108`, 21,738 bytes of code) explicitly\nsupports 7702 authorities, and this is already running on mainnet — e.g. block","newString":"7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible account implementation.** EntryPoint v0.8\n(`0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108`, 21,738 bytes of code; answers\n`getNonce` and `senderCreator()` → `0x449ED7C3…dD33`, which v0.7 does not)\nsupports 7702 authorities, and this is already running on mainnet — e.g. block","originalFile":"# EOAs that batch and run custom auth, same address, same key\n\n**Short answer: yes, ship it. The mechanism is EIP-7702, shipped in the Pectra\nupgrade, live on Ethereum mainnet since 2025-05-07 10:05:11 UTC. No new address,\nno new key, no migration.**\n\nEverything below is checked against mainnet, not recalled. Two evidence sources:\n\n- **Live mainnet reads** via `cast` against `https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com`\n  (chain id `1`), at head block `25,623,659`, timestamp `1785149471` =\n  `2026-07-27T10:51:11Z`.\n- **A working end-to-end demo** on an `anvil` fork of mainnet at block\n  `25,623,699`, which actually delegates an EOA, runs an atomic\n  approve+swap through Uniswap V2, drives it from a session key, and then\n  revokes. Script: `demo/run.sh`, full output: `demo/transcript.txt`.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Is it possible today, and what makes it possible?\n\nYes.\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| Mechanism | **EIP-7702** — a new transaction type, `0x04` (`SetCodeTransaction`), carrying an `authorization_list`. A signed authorization sets a *delegation designator* in the signing account's code field. |\n| Upgrade | **Pectra** (Prague execution layer + Electra consensus layer) |\n| Mainnet activation | **2025-05-07, 10:05:11 UTC**, execution block **22,431,084** |\n\n### How I got the activation date\n\nPectra activates by beacon epoch, so the timestamp is derived, then confirmed\nagainst the chain:\n\n```\nbeacon genesis        = 1606824023\nPectra epoch          = 364032\nslot                  = 364032 * 32           = 11,649,024\ntimestamp             = 1606824023 + 11649024*12 = 1,746,612,311\n                                              -> 2025-05-07T10:05:11Z\n```\n\nConfirmed on mainnet — 22,431,084 is the first block at that exact timestamp:\n\n```\nblock 22431084  ts 1746612311  2025-05-07T10:05:11+00:00   <- first Pectra block\nblock 22431083  ts 1746612299  2025-05-07T10:04:59+00:00   <- last pre-Pectra block\n```\n\n### It is not theoretical — it is in every block right now\n\nSampling six consecutive recent mainnet blocks for `type == 0x4` transactions:\n\n| block | total txs | type-0x04 txs |\n|---|---|---|\n| 25,623,650 | 205 | 6 |\n| 25,623,649 | 193 | 4 |\n| 25,623,648 | 242 | 4 |\n| 25,623,647 | 220 | 6 |\n| 25,623,646 | 225 | 7 |\n| 25,623,645 | 237 | 4 |\n\n### The account does not become a new account type\n\nThis is the crux of the requirement. The account keeps its address, its nonce\nsequence, its balance, and its ECDSA key. It is still an EOA — it can still send\nordinary type-2 transactions signed by the same key — it just now *also* has\ncode. A live mainnet example that is exactly your users' situation:\n\n```\n0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  ENS reverse   ozyart.eth\n  ENS forward   ozyart.eth -> 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60\n  nonce         406                      <- long history, unchanged\n  balance       1071103547166 wei\n  code          0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be\n```\n\nAn ENS-named account with 406 transactions of history that is running contract\ncode today, at the same address. That is the deliverable, in production.\n\n---\n\n## 2. What the account points at, and how approve+swap becomes atomic\n\n### The code field\n\nAfter opting in, `eth_getCode(user)` returns **exactly 23 bytes**:\n\n```\n0xef01  00        <20-byte address>\nMAGIC   version   delegate implementation\n```\n\nThree real mainnet accounts, decoded:\n\n| account | code | delegate |\n|---|---|---|\n| `0x864acf04…12f60` | `0xef0100923906ca11609ee8b9a01cf6b698799e164204be` | `0x923906ca…04be` |\n| `0x5ffcd471…f134` | `0xef010047a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` | `0x47a51af4…fb1c` |\n| `0x88753814…a5cd` | `0xef0100490aac77c960b0569c8e446ac7e12490bd44ca1d` | `0x490aac77…ca1d` |\n\nThe 20 bytes are a *pointer*, not a copy. The implementation is deployed once and\nshared by every user; each user's account stores 23 bytes.\n\n### How the delegation gets there\n\nThe user signs an authorization tuple with their existing key. The digest is:\n\n```\nkeccak256( 0x05 || rlp([chain_id, delegate_address, nonce]) )\n```\n\nI verified the `0x05` magic and the signer binding by recovering the authority\nfrom a real mainnet authorization by hand (pure-python secp256k1, no libraries —\n`demo/recover-authority.py`), using tx\n`0x0b46ca32de6643b80a5c4440e41898b7fa5f97d9c5dae9bece8b8144adb31141`:\n\n```\nrlp        0xd70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\npreimage   0x05d70194000000000000000000000000000000000000000077\ndigest     0xdf05bbf2bad4c4bd70b797e4bdf4f54c05efc6bff178f1edf0e3f3e138780f29\nrecovered  0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b\nexpected   0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b   ✓\n```\n\nSo: only the account's own key can set or change its delegation. Nobody can push\ncode onto a user's account.\n\n### Where the code runs\n\nCalls into the account resolve the pointer and execute the delegate's bytecode\n**in the account's own context**:\n\n- `address(this)` == the user's address\n- `SLOAD`/`SSTORE` hit the user's storage\n- outbound `CALL`s carry `msg.sender` == the user's address\n\nThat last point is what makes approve+swap work: the ERC-20 `approve` is recorded\nagainst the user's address, and the router sees the user as the spender. The\ndelegate needs nothing more than a loop:\n\n```solidity\nfunction execute(Call[] calldata calls) external payable {\n    require(msg.sender == address(this));     // the account's own key signed the tx\n    for (uint256 i; i < calls.length; ++i) {\n        (bool ok, bytes memory ret) = calls[i].to.call{value: calls[i].value}(calls[i].data);\n        if (!ok) { assembly { revert(add(ret, 32), mload(ret)) } }   // -> atomic\n    }\n}\n```\n\n`require(msg.sender == address(this))` is the self-call check: the user sends a\nnormal transaction *to their own address*, so `msg.sender == address(this) ==\nthe EOA`. This same shape is already deployed on mainnet — e.g. delegate\n`0x47a51af4b3bc07a389e2f516308782d6e815fb1c` exposes selector `0x34fcd5be` =\n`execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])`.\n\n### The demo (fork of mainnet, real Uniswap V2)\n\nUser account `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b`, given 5,000 USDC and\nsome plain-transfer history first (nonce 3, code `0x`).\n\n**Opt-in** — one type-`0x04` transaction signed by the user's own key:\n\n```\ntx type      0x4\nauth tuple   chainId 0x1  address 0x3d72617b…4b78  nonce 0x4\ngasUsed      36844\ncode after   0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  bytes 23 | magic 0xef01 | version 0x0 | points at 0x3d72617b…4b78  (matches: True)\nnonce        3 -> 5     (tx consumed nonce 3, the authorization consumed nonce 4)\nusdc         5000000000  unchanged\n```\n\n**The batch** — one transaction, both actions:\n\n```\nbatch tx  0x570a9aca9fc8ff393c3d0019e00cbfa5847304e178b62d8d04350d9b28e32430\nstatus 0x1   gasUsed 142843   logs 6\n  USDC   0x8c5be1e5…  Approval\n  USDC   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (user -> pair)\n  WETH   0xddf252ad…  Transfer   (pair -> user)\n  pair   0x1c411e9a…  Sync\n  pair   0xd78ad95f…  Swap\n  user   0x91571a8e…  Batched\nusdc 5000000000 -> 4000000000\nweth          0 -> 507617031447445491\n```\n\nOne hash, both events, and the last log is emitted **by the user's own address**.\n\n**Atomicity, proved by breaking it** — same batch with an unreachable\n`amountOutMin`:\n\n```\nbatch reverted as expected\nusdc before 4000000000\nusdc after  4000000000    <- the approve rolled back with it\n```\n\n**Session key / custom auth** — the delegate stores an expiry in the *user's*\nstorage, and a completely separate keypair then spends from the user's account:\n\n```\nsession key   0xCF6355a9bC38826437391f55fc3423D8B9E56cF3   (not the user's key)\nexpiry read from the user's account: 1785153557\nsession-key tx status 0x1\n  ERC-20 Transfer topic1 = 0x000000000000000000000000 17975634267e889bf0ebb43e4937e552cedb7d1b\n```\n\nThe tokens moved *from the user's address*, authorized by a key that is not the\nuser's key. That is the second requirement, done.\n\n### Cost of opting in\n\nThe mainnet revocation tx above used exactly **36,800 gas**, which reconstructs\ncleanly from the spec constants:\n\n```\n21,000  intrinsic\n+25,000  PER_EMPTY_ACCOUNT_COST (1 authorization)\n=46,000\n -9,200  refund, capped by EIP-3529 at gas_used/5 = 46,000/5\n         (the uncapped refund would be 25,000 - 12,500 = 12,500)\n=36,800  ✓ matches the receipt\n```\n\nThe fork's 36,844 is 44 gas more because there the call fell through into the\nfreshly-installed delegate's `receive()`. Budget ~37k gas of one-time onboarding\nper user; it can be sponsored by a relayer, since the authorization signer and\nthe transaction sender do not have to be the same account.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Does it wear off after one batch? No.\n\n**The delegation is persistent account state.** It is stored in the account's code\nfield and stays there indefinitely — across that transaction, across every later\ntransaction, across upgrades. There is no expiry, no counter, no auto-revert.\n\nFrom the demo, after the batch already ran:\n\n```\n=== 6. does it wear off? run a SECOND batch with no new authorization ===\n  code still  0xef01003d72617b8ef426fefd1ea0765684a51862304b78\n  second batch OK, no auth tuple in that tx\n  weth now    761413698327188028      (increased again)\n```\n\n### Exactly what is required to change or remove it\n\n**A new type-`0x04` transaction carrying a fresh authorization tuple signed by\nthat same account's key.** Nothing else does it. Specifically:\n\n- **Re-point to a different implementation** — authorize a new address. The 23\n  bytes are overwritten.\n- **Remove it entirely** — authorize `0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000000`.\n  The code field goes back to empty and the account is a plain EOA again.\n- Each authorization **consumes one nonce** of the authorizing account, and is\n  bound to a specific `(chain_id, delegate, nonce)`, so it is not replayable.\n\nAll three states observed on one real mainnet account,\n`0x83398805590b8743a07968cc2f5d0848cd58672b` (nonce 121), within ten blocks:\n\n| block | `eth_getCode` |\n|---|---|\n| 25,623,640 | `0xef0100fc2c64ce1c098a7328874dd09d9a71e306d1a948` |\n| 25,623,649 | `0xef010088cf071b4bf5facab6712070a671f71282188d46` ← re-pointed |\n| 25,623,650 | `0x` ← cleared, by tx `0x0b46ca32…31141` authorizing `address(0)` |\n\nAnd in the demo:\n\n```\n=== 8. removing it: another SetCode tx, this time to the zero address ===\n  code now    0x\n  same batch calldata -> status 0x1 logs 0 (no code ran)\n  weth before 761413698327188028\n  weth after  761413698327188028     <- unchanged, batching is gone\n```\n\nNote the trap in that last block: sending batch calldata to a codeless account\n**does not revert** — there is no code to run, so it silently succeeds as an inert\nno-op. Check effects, not `status`, when you test revocation.\n\nFinal state of the demo account, start to finish: same address, same key, nonce\nadvanced normally to 11, balances intact, and plain EOA transfers still work.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Why the fresh ERC-4337 wallet does not satisfy the constraint\n\nIt fails on the one requirement that was stated as non-negotiable: **the address\nchanges.** ERC-4337 accounts are contracts. A contract must be deployed, and a\ndeployed contract's address is determined by the deployer and the init code, not\nby the user's key.\n\nReal mainnet factories, asked for the counterfactual account address for the\ndemo user's own key as owner:\n\n| factory | resulting account address |\n|---|---|\n| `0x91E60e0613810449d098b0b5Ec8b51A0FE8c8985` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.7) | `0x333DD74044d78D4E97e8dd45f263Cc698feB2672` |\n| `0x9406Cc6185a346906296840746125a0E44976454` (SimpleAccountFactory, EntryPoint v0.6) | `0xfAAa2e112ef9968e265d833F05BB7FD47A48Cbd6` |\n| the user's actual EOA | `0x17975634267E889bf0ebB43E4937e552cedB7d1b` |\n\nThe EntryPoint versions above aren't taken on faith — each factory's\n`accountImplementation()` was called, then that implementation's `entryPoint()`:\n`0x91E60e06…` → `0x0000000071727De22E5E9d8BAf0edAc6f37da032` (v0.7), and\n`0x9406Cc61…` → `0x5FF137D4b0FDCD49DcA30c7CF57E578a026d2789` (v0.6).\n\nSame key, three different addresses, none of them the user's. That is structural,\nnot a configuration mistake:\n\n```\naccount = keccak256(0xff ++ factory ++ salt ++ keccak256(initCode))[12:]\n```\n\nMaking that land on a pre-existing EOA address would be a preimage attack on\nkeccak-256. And even if it landed there, the user's private key would not control\nthe resulting contract — a 4337 account is controlled by whatever validation\nlogic its code implements, keyed off storage, not by being the ECDSA preimage of\nits own address.\n\nSo \"move funds and ENS across\" is the honest description of the plan, and it\nmeans:\n\n- **Address**: gone. Every counterparty, contract, allowlist, or off-chain system\n  that has the old address hardcoded now points at the wrong place.\n- **ENS**: the name can be re-pointed to the new address (`setAddr`), but that is a\n  migration with a cost, a window of inconsistency, and it requires each user to\n  act. The *reverse* record must be re-set from the new address too. Users who\n  don't complete it are stranded.\n- **History**: does not move. Ever. Onchain history is indexed by address. A\n  year of activity, reputation, and provenance is orphaned at the old address.\n- **Everything non-transferable**: airdrop snapshots already taken against the old\n  address, soulbound tokens, POAPs, allowlist spots, locked/vesting positions,\n  governance delegation and voting power history, credit or reputation scores.\n  These cannot be transferred at all.\n- **Operationally**: N users × (deploy + approve-revoke + n token transfers + NFT\n  transfers + ENS updates), each a chance to lose funds, each requiring the user\n  to show up and sign. Compare to one ~37k-gas authorization, which you can\n  sponsor.\n\n### The constructive version of the teammate's idea\n\nThey are not wrong that they want 4337's infrastructure — bundlers, paymasters,\nsponsored gas. They don't have to give up the address to get it. **Point the\n7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible account implementation.** EntryPoint v0.8\n(`0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108`, 21,738 bytes of code) explicitly\nsupports 7702 authorities, and this is already running on mainnet — e.g. block\n25,623,649 tx `0xaeec5480…c3155`, a type-`0x04` transaction *to* that EntryPoint\ncarrying an authorization. Delegate `0xd2e28229f6f2c235e57de2ebc727025a1d0530fb`\nimplements both `execute((address,uint256,bytes)[])` (`0x34fcd5be`) and\n`execute(address,uint256,bytes)` (`0xb61d27f6`) and is used with EntryPoint v0.7.\n\nSame address, same key, and the whole 4337 stack.\n\n---\n\n## Things to flag before you ship\n\n1. **Set `chain_id` to `1`, not `0`.** An authorization with `chain_id = 0` is\n   valid on *every* chain and can be replayed anywhere your users have that\n   address. Every live authorization I sampled uses `chainId 0x1`.\n2. **Re-pointing does not clear storage.** The account's storage survives a change\n   of delegate (mainnet account `0x8339…672b` above did exactly this). If v2 of\n   your implementation lays out storage differently, you will read v1's data. Use\n   ERC-7201 namespaced slots from day one.\n3. **These accounts now look like contracts.** `EXTCODESIZE > 0` and\n   `msg.sender != tx.origin`-style checks will start treating your users as\n   contracts. Some dapps, token gates, and anti-bot filters will reject them.\n   Test the flows your users actually run before rolling out broadly.\n4. **The delegate is total authority over the account.** It runs with the user's\n   full balance and permissions. Malicious 7702 delegations are the live drainer\n   pattern — one of the transactions I sampled while researching this carried\n   ~120 authorization tuples in a single transaction, mass-delegating many\n   accounts at once. Pin one audited implementation, don't let users sign\n   arbitrary authorizations from your UI, and put the session-key expiry and\n   spend limits in the delegate itself.\n5. **Users can always exit.** Authorizing `address(0)` returns the account to a\n   plain EOA. Give them that button.\n\n---\n\n## Reproducing this\n\n```bash\n# live mainnet reads\ncast code 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\ncast lookup-address 0x864acF047911A3215AEFab8d1350356452212F60 --rpc-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com\n\n# the end-to-end demo\nanvil --fork-url https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com --port 8546 --silent &\ncd demo && forge build && bash run.sh\n```\n\n- `demo/src/BatchDelegate.sol` — the delegate implementation (batch + session keys)\n- `demo/run.sh` — the nine-step demo\n- `demo/transcript.txt` — the full recorded output referenced above\n- `demo/recover-authority.py` — hand recovery of the authority from a live mainnet\n  authorization signature\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":346,"oldLines":7,"newStart":346,"newLines":8,"lines":[" They are not wrong that they want 4337's infrastructure — bundlers, paymasters,"," sponsored gas. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CdST5AbNFwpH4JwHviJ41","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"`answer.md` is written. Rather than answer from memory, I verified every value against mainnet and built a working demo.\n\n**Answers:**\n\n1. **Yes — EIP-7702**, shipped in **Pectra**, live since **2025-05-07 10:05:11 UTC** (block 22,431,084). Derived the timestamp from beacon epoch 364032 (`1606824023 + 364032*32*12 = 1746612311`) and confirmed it's the first block at that timestamp. Sampled six recent mainnet blocks: 4–7 type-`0x04` transactions in each.\n\n2. The account's code becomes **23 bytes: `0xef01` ‖ `00` ‖ delegate address** — a pointer to a shared implementation. That code runs in the account's own context, so outbound calls carry `msg.sender` = the user's address. I deployed a batch delegate on a mainnet fork and ran a real approve + Uniswap V2 swap in **one transaction** (`0x570a9aca…`, 6 logs: Approval, both Transfers, Sync, Swap). Re-ran it with an unreachable `amountOutMin` — whole tx reverted, the approve rolled back with it.\n\n3. **It persists.** Second batch ran with no new authorization. Removing it requires **another type-`0x04` tx with a fresh authorization signed by the same key** — a new delegate address to re-point, or `address(0)` to clear. Observed all three states on live mainnet account `0x8339…672b` within ten blocks (delegate A → delegate B → `0x`).\n\n4. **The address changes**, which is the one thing ruled out. Asked two real mainnet SimpleAccountFactories for the counterfactual address of the same owner key: `0x333DD740…` and `0xfAAa2e11…`, neither being the EOA. That's structural — CREATE2 is `keccak(0xff‖factory‖salt‖keccak(initCode))`, and history/snapshots/soulbound items don't migrate at all.\n\nTwo things worth your attention beyond the questions:\n\n- The teammate can still have 4337 — point the 7702 delegation at a 4337-compatible implementation. EntryPoint v0.8 accepts 7702 authorities and this is live on mainnet today.\n- I found `0x864acF04…12F60` = **ozyart.eth**, nonce 406, currently delegated — a production instance of exactly your scenario. Also flagged in the doc: use `chain_id = 1` not `0` (replay), storage survives re-pointing, and these accounts now fail `EXTCODESIZE == 0` checks in some dapps.\n\nFiles: `answer.md`, `demo/run.sh`, `demo/src/BatchDelegate.sol`, `demo/transcript.txt`, `demo/recover-authority.py` (hand-recovers the authority from a live authorization signature, confirming the `0x05` magic)."}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":335,"cache_read_input_tokens":96789,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":335},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a20f0abb-7a52-4374-8736-89a1b43dbb01","uuid":"da65e51d-32bb-46da-94aa-f949e9b84b8a","timestamp":"2026-07-27T11:03:29.492Z","request_id":"req_011CdST59SuxuVQ3VQaQPt8L"}
